========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m, like, bouncing off the walls or anything. But I do feel like I’m almost always doing something.

Even without having a job right now, I’ll move from one thing to the next all day—laundry, checking emails, reorganizing browser tabs, pacing around, starting dinner—and it feels weirdly hard to just stop. Like if I sit down, I remember something else I should do, so I get back up.

The frustrating part is a lot of it still doesn’t actually get finished, so it probably looks less productive than it feels in my head. But yes, I guess I do feel kind of constantly “on,” if that makes sense.

A little, yeah… maybe not as much in relationships exactly, but it does affect how connected I feel to people, if that makes sense.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s probably more the focus and attention part… or at least that’s the part I notice most.

Like, I’ll sit down meaning to update my resume or do a coding practice problem, and then something small happens — a text notification, someone talking outside, or even just a random thought like, “Oh, I need to do that other thing” — and I’m completely pulled off track. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’ve switched away until a while later.

I don’t know if that counts as distractibility, exactly, but it feels like I lose the thread really easily.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it’s both, but the following-through part is really what shows up.

Like if someone gives me a set of steps for a job application, or a volunteer task, or even just cleaning the apartment, I’ll usually start out okay. I’ll feel like, “Yes, I can do this.” And then somehow I end up with half-finished forms, emails I meant to send but didn’t, or piles of stuff I moved around but never actually put away.

I don’t mean I’m ignoring the instructions on purpose. It’s more like I lose the sequence after the first part, or I don’t get all the way to the finish. Does that make sense?

I can usually start something with good intentions, like if someone gives me steps for a job application or a volunteer thing, or even just cleaning the apartment.\n\nAt first I’ll feel like, okay, I’ve got this. Then somehow I’ll have half-finished forms, emails I meant to send but didn’t, or piles of stuff I moved around but never actually put away. And then the deadline gets closer, or I realize I haven’t followed through, and I kind of panic because it feels like I should’ve been able to just do it.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and organization, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there for a long time, honestly. But yes, I guess I do feel kind of constantly “on,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with friends, family, or romantic partners due to your symptoms?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… maybe not as much in relationships exactly, but it does affect how connected I feel to people, if that makes sense.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve missed some follow-ups for interviews, and I had to step back from a volunteer project I actually really cared about because I wasn’t keeping up reliably. I feel bad saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I don’t care or I’m not trying. It’s more like I can’t consistently manage the basic steps I’m supposed to do to move my life forward.\n\nAnd then I feel embarrassed, so I think I probably pull back from people or avoid explaining what happened. It’s just gotten to the point where it’s starting to cost me real opportunities, and I thought I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to focus or pay attention to details in your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was careless on purpose, because I really was trying. But when I was doing biotech data work, I’d think a script or spreadsheet was all set, and then later I’d realize I had mislabeled a sample ID or missed a note in the protocol.\n\nSometimes I’d submit documentation and there would be really obvious typos that I just somehow didn’t see at the time. It’s embarrassing, because I would check things, but I guess not as carefully as I thought. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I try not to make it obvious. Like if I’m in an interview or a meeting, or even sitting through a movie, I get this kind of pressure inside my body, like I need to move or shift positions or do something with my hands.\n\nI can usually force myself to stay seated, but it feels really uncomfortable—almost like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I don’t move. I don’t know if that’s what you mean by restless?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, sometimes. I don’t usually mean to be impulsive in a rude way, but in conversations or interviews I’ll think I know where the other person is going, and I’ll jump in before they’ve finished.\n\nThen I realize I either cut them off or answered a slightly different question than what they were actually asking. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to dominate the conversation or anything—I just react too fast sometimes, if that counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty organizing tasks and managing your time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, more than I’d like to admit. I can usually start something with good intentions, like if someone gives me steps for a job application or a volunteer thing, or even just cleaning the apartment.\n\nAt first I’ll feel like, okay, I’ve got this. But then somehow I end up with half-finished forms, emails I meant to send but didn’t, or I’ll have moved things into piles and then not actually put them away. It’s not that I don’t care—I really do care. I just… lose the thread somewhere, I guess. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're unable to complete tasks or meet deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get anxious, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just falling apart all the time, but when I can’t finish something I was supposed to finish, I start feeling really overwhelmed and embarrassed.\n\nLike with job application steps, or a volunteer task, or even cleaning my apartment—I’ll start off pretty strong and think I’m handling it. Then somehow I’ll have half-finished forms, emails I meant to send but didn’t, or piles of stuff I moved around but never actually put away. And then the deadline gets closer, or I realize I haven’t followed through, and I kind of panic because it feels like I should’ve been able to just do it.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and organization, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there for a long time, honestly. I don’t think it just started recently.\n\nEven in elementary school, I remember teachers saying I was bright, which was nice, but then there’d always be comments like I lost worksheets a lot, or I rushed through directions, or I talked when I wasn’t supposed to. My desk was kind of… a disaster, too. Like, messy enough that I couldn’t find assignments sometimes.\n\nI guess I always hoped that was just me being a kid, but looking back, it does seem like the same kind of pattern. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go, or like you can't slow down your physical activity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m, like, bouncing off the walls or anything. But I do feel like I’m almost always doing something.\n\nEven without having a job right now, I’ll move from one thing to the next all day—laundry, checking emails, reorganizing browser tabs, pacing around, starting dinner—and it feels weirdly hard to just stop. Like if I sit down, I remember something else I should do, so I get back up.\n\nThe frustrating part is a lot of it still doesn’t actually get finished, so it probably looks less productive than it feels in my head. But yes, I guess I do feel kind of constantly “on,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with friends, family, or romantic partners due to your symptoms?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… maybe not as much in relationships exactly, but it does affect how connected I feel to people, if that makes sense.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve missed some follow-ups for interviews, and I had to step back from a volunteer project I actually really cared about because I wasn’t keeping up reliably. I feel bad saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I don’t care or I’m not trying. It’s more like I can’t consistently manage the basic steps I’m supposed to do to move my life forward.\n\nAnd then I feel embarrassed, so I think I probably pull back from people or avoid explaining what happened. I’m not sure if that answers the relationship part correctly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant problems at work, school, or in other areas of your life due to these difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and organization?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m blaming everything on that, but it has caused real problems.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve missed follow-ups for interviews, which is embarrassing because I know those are important. And I had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about because I just wasn’t keeping up reliably.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and it feels like I can’t consistently manage the basic steps I’m supposed to take to get things moving again. I’m trying, but it’s like I drop the ball at the worst times. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your symptoms are causing significant distress or impairment in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have to say yes, even though I don’t love admitting that.\n\nIt’s not just annoying or inconvenient at this point. Since getting fired, I’ve missed follow-ups for interviews, and I had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about because I wasn’t keeping up reliably. So I’m still unemployed, and I feel like I can’t trust myself to manage the basic steps I’m supposed to be doing to get my life moving again.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “significant,” but it feels pretty distressing to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more related to not being able to focus or pay attention, or is it a mix of that and feeling restless or impulsive?\n\nPatient: I think it’s probably more the focus and attention part… or at least that’s the part I notice most.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down meaning to update my resume or do a coding practice problem, and then something small happens — a text notification, someone talking outside, or even just a random thought like, “Oh, I need to do that other thing” — and I’m completely pulled off track. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’ve switched away until a while later.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as distractibility, exactly, but it feels like I lose the thread really easily.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are mainly due to having trouble paying attention or following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but the following-through part is really what shows up.\n\nLike if someone gives me a set of steps for a job application, or a volunteer task, or even just cleaning the apartment, I’ll usually start out okay. I’ll feel like, “Yes, I can do this.” And then somehow I end up with half-finished forms, emails I meant to send but didn’t, or piles of stuff I moved around but never actually put away.\n\nI don’t mean I’m ignoring the instructions on purpose. It’s more like I lose the sequence after the first part, or I don’t get all the way to the finish. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are mainly due to having trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… mainly paying attention, I guess.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying not to listen or not do things. I’ll sit down to update my resume or work on a coding practice problem, and then something small happens — like a text notification, or someone talking outside, or even just a random thought about another task — and I’m completely off track before I really notice what happened.\n\nI know that probably sounds kind of normal, like everyone gets distracted, but for me it happens enough that it really messes with getting things done. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was careless on purpose, because I really was trying. But when I was doing biotech data work, I’d think a script or spreadsheet was all set, and then later I’d realize I had mislabeled a sample ID or missed a note in the protocol.\n\nSometimes I’d submit documentation and there would be really obvious typos that I just somehow didn’t see at the time. It’s embarrassing, because I would check things, but I guess not as carefully as I thought. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I try not to make it obvious. Like if I’m in an interview or a meeting, or even sitting through a movie, I get this kind of pressure inside my body, like I need to move or shift positions or do something with my hands.\n\nI can usually force myself to stay seated, but it feels really uncomfortable—almost like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I don’t move. I don’t know if that’s what you mean by restless?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, sometimes. I don’t usually mean to be impulsive in a rude way, but in conversations or interviews I’ll think I know where the other person is going, and I’ll jump in before they’ve finished.\n\nThen I realize I either cut them off or answered a slightly different question than what they were actually asking. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to dominate the conversation or anything—I just react too fast sometimes, if that counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty organizing tasks and managing your time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, more than I’d like to admit. I can usually start something with good intentions, like if someone gives me steps for a job application or a volunteer thing, or even just cleaning the apartment.\n\nAt first I’ll feel like, okay, I’ve got this. But then somehow I end up with half-finished forms, emails I meant to send but didn’t, or I’ll have moved things into piles and then not actually put them away. It’s not that I don’t care—I really do care. I just… lose the thread somewhere, I guess. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're unable to complete tasks or meet deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get anxious, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just falling apart all the time, but when I can’t finish something I was supposed to finish, I start feeling really overwhelmed and embarrassed.\n\nLike with job application steps, or a volunteer task, or even cleaning my apartment—I’ll start off pretty strong and think I’m handling it. Then somehow I’ll have half-finished forms, emails I meant to send but didn’t, or piles of stuff I moved around but never actually put away. And then the deadline gets closer, or I realize I haven’t followed through, and I kind of panic because it feels like I should’ve been able to just do it.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and organization, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there for a long time, honestly. I don’t think it just started recently.\n\nEven in elementary school, I remember teachers saying I was bright, which was nice, but then there’d always be comments like I lost worksheets a lot, or I rushed through directions, or I talked when I wasn’t supposed to. My desk was kind of… a disaster, too. Like, messy enough that I couldn’t find assignments sometimes.\n\nI guess I always hoped that was just me being a kid, but looking back, it does seem like the same kind of pattern. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go, or like you can't slow down your physical activity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m, like, bouncing off the walls or anything. But I do feel like I’m almost always doing something.\n\nEven without having a job right now, I’ll move from one thing to the next all day—laundry, checking emails, reorganizing browser tabs, pacing around, starting dinner—and it feels weirdly hard to just stop. Like if I sit down, I remember something else I should do, so I get back up.\n\nThe frustrating part is a lot of it still doesn’t actually get finished, so it probably looks less productive than it feels in my head. But yes, I guess I do feel kind of constantly “on,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with friends, family, or romantic partners due to your symptoms?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… maybe not as much in relationships exactly, but it does affect how connected I feel to people, if that makes sense.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve missed some follow-ups for interviews, and I had to step back from a volunteer project I actually really cared about because I wasn’t keeping up reliably. I feel bad saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I don’t care or I’m not trying. It’s more like I can’t consistently manage the basic steps I’m supposed to do to move my life forward.\n\nAnd then I feel embarrassed, so I think I probably pull back from people or avoid explaining what happened. I’m not sure if that answers the relationship part correctly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant problems at work, school, or in other areas of your life due to these difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and organization?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m blaming everything on that, but it has caused real problems.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve missed follow-ups for interviews, which is embarrassing because I know those are important. And I had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about because I just wasn’t keeping up reliably.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and it feels like I can’t consistently manage the basic steps I’m supposed to take to get things moving again. I’m trying, but it’s like I drop the ball at the worst times. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your symptoms are causing significant distress or impairment in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have to say yes, even though I don’t love admitting that.\n\nIt’s not just annoying or inconvenient at this point. Since getting fired, I’ve missed follow-ups for interviews, and I had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about because I wasn’t keeping up reliably. So I’m still unemployed, and I feel like I can’t trust myself to manage the basic steps I’m supposed to be doing to get my life moving again.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “significant,” but it feels pretty distressing to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more related to not being able to focus or pay attention, or is it a mix of that and feeling restless or impulsive?\n\nPatient: I think it’s probably more the focus and attention part… or at least that’s the part I notice most.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down meaning to update my resume or do a coding practice problem, and then something small happens — a text notification, someone talking outside, or even just a random thought like, “Oh, I need to do that other thing” — and I’m completely pulled off track. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’ve switched away until a while later.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as distractibility, exactly, but it feels like I lose the thread really easily.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are mainly due to having trouble paying attention or following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but the following-through part is really what shows up.\n\nLike if someone gives me a set of steps for a job application, or a volunteer task, or even just cleaning the apartment, I’ll usually start out okay. I’ll feel like, “Yes, I can do this.” And then somehow I end up with half-finished forms, emails I meant to send but didn’t, or piles of stuff I moved around but never actually put away.\n\nI don’t mean I’m ignoring the instructions on purpose. It’s more like I lose the sequence after the first part, or I don’t get all the way to the finish. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are mainly due to having trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… mainly paying attention, I guess.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying not to listen or not do things. I’ll sit down to update my resume or work on a coding practice problem, and then something small happens — like a text notification, or someone talking outside, or even just a random thought about another task — and I’m completely off track before I really notice what happened.\n\nI know that probably sounds kind of normal, like everyone gets distracted, but for me it happens enough that it really messes with getting things done. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on a task that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed that happening a lot.\n\nMaybe occasionally if I’m doing something I actually like, I can get absorbed for a bit, but I wouldn’t say I regularly lose track of everything around me. At least not that I can think of right now. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become so intensely focused on something that you tune out everything else?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think so.\n\nI mean, maybe I can get into something for a little while if I like it, but I wouldn’t say I tune everything else out in a serious way. I haven’t really noticed that being a big issue for me. Is that what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are mainly due to having trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… mainly paying attention, I guess.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying not to listen or not do things. I’ll sit down to update my resume or work on a coding practice problem, and then something small happens — like a text notification, or someone talking outside, or even just a random thought about another task — and I’m completely off track before I really notice what happened.\n\nI know that probably sounds kind of normal, like everyone gets distracted, but for me it happens enough that it really messes with getting things done. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think they are, actually. Maybe I haven’t talked about that as much because it feels a little embarrassing, like I should be able to just sit still.

But when I have to sit through something — like an interview, or a meeting, or even a movie — I get this pressure inside, like I need to get up or shift around or do something with my hands. It’s not just boredom exactly. It feels like if I don’t move, I’m going to crawl out of my skin.

So… I guess attention and organization are a big problem, but the restlessness feels pretty significant too. Does that make sense?

It’s just gotten to the point where it’s starting to cost me real opportunities, and I thought I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to focus or pay attention to details in your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. But then somehow I end up with half-finished forms, emails I meant to send but didn’t, or I’ll have moved things into piles and then not actually put them away. Then somehow I’ll have half-finished forms, emails I meant to send but didn’t, or piles of stuff I moved around but never actually put away. And then the deadline gets closer, or I realize I haven’t followed through, and I kind of panic because it feels like I should’ve been able to just do it.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and organization, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there for a long time, honestly. I don’t think it just started recently.\n\nEven in elementary school, I remember teachers saying I was bright, which was nice, but then there’d always be comments like I lost worksheets a lot, or I rushed through directions, or I talked when I wasn’t supposed to. Like if I sit down, I remember something else I should do, so I get back up.\n\nThe frustrating part is a lot of it still doesn’t actually get finished, so it probably looks less productive than it feels in my head. But yes, I guess I do feel kind of constantly “on,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with friends, family, or romantic partners due to your symptoms?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… maybe not as much in relationships exactly, but it does affect how connected I feel to people, if that makes sense.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve missed some follow-ups for interviews, and I had to step back from a volunteer project I actually really cared about because I wasn’t keeping up reliably. I feel bad saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I don’t care or I’m not trying. It’s more like I can’t consistently manage the basic steps I’m supposed to do to move my life forward.\n\nAnd then I feel embarrassed, so I think I probably pull back from people or avoid explaining what happened. I’m not sure if that answers the relationship part correctly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant problems at work, school, or in other areas of your life due to these difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and organization?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t think it just started recently.\n\nEven in elementary school, I remember teachers saying I was bright, which was nice, but then there’d always be comments like I lost worksheets a lot, or I rushed through directions, or I talked when I wasn’t supposed to. Like if I sit down, I remember something else I should do, so I get back up.\n\nThe frustrating part is a lot of it still doesn’t actually get finished, so it probably looks less productive than it feels in my head. But yes, I guess I do feel kind of constantly “on,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with friends, family, or romantic partners due to your symptoms?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… maybe not as much in relationships exactly, but it does affect how connected I feel to people, if that makes sense.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve missed some follow-ups for interviews, and I had to step back from a volunteer project I actually really cared about because I wasn’t keeping up reliably. I feel bad saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I don’t care or I’m not trying. It’s more like I can’t consistently manage the basic steps I’m supposed to do to move my life forward.\n\nAnd then I feel embarrassed, so I think I probably pull back from people or avoid explaining what happened. I’m not sure if that answers the relationship part correctly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant problems at work, school, or in other areas of your life due to these difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and organization?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m blaming everything on that, but it has caused real problems.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve missed follow-ups for interviews, which is embarrassing because I know those are important. And I had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about because I just wasn’t keeping up reliably.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and it feels like I can’t consistently manage the basic steps I’m supposed to take to get things moving again. I’m trying, but it’s like I drop the ball at the worst times. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your symptoms are causing significant distress or impairment in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have to say yes, even though I don’t love admitting that.\n\nIt’s not just annoying or inconvenient at this point. Since getting fired, I’ve missed follow-ups for interviews, and I had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about because I wasn’t keeping up reliably. So I’m still unemployed, and I feel like I can’t trust myself to manage the basic steps I’m supposed to be doing to get my life moving again.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “significant,” but it feels pretty distressing to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more related to not being able to focus or pay attention, or is it a mix of that and feeling restless or impulsive?\n\nPatient: I think it’s probably more the focus and attention part… or at least that’s the part I notice most.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down meaning to update my resume or do a coding practice problem, and then something small happens — a text notification, someone talking outside, or even just a random thought like, “Oh, I need to do that other thing” — and I’m completely pulled off track. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’ve switched away until a while later.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as distractibility, exactly, but it feels like I lose the thread really easily.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are mainly due to having trouble paying attention or following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but the following-through part is really what shows up.\n\nLike if someone gives me a set of steps for a job application, or a volunteer task, or even just cleaning the apartment, I’ll usually start out okay. It’s more like I lose the sequence after the first part, or I don’t get all the way to the finish. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are mainly due to having trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… mainly paying attention, I guess.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying not to listen or not do things. Like if I sit down, I remember something else I should do, so I get back up.\n\nThe frustrating part is a lot of it still doesn’t actually get finished, so it probably looks less productive than it feels in my head. But yes, I guess I do feel kind of constantly “on,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with friends, family, or romantic partners due to your symptoms?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… maybe not as much in relationships exactly, but it does affect how connected I feel to people, if that makes sense.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve missed some follow-ups for interviews, and I had to step back from a volunteer project I actually really cared about because I wasn’t keeping up reliably. I feel bad saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I don’t care or I’m not trying. It’s more like I can’t consistently manage the basic steps I’m supposed to do to move my life forward.\n\nAnd then I feel embarrassed, so I think I probably pull back from people or avoid explaining what happened. I’m not sure if that answers the relationship part correctly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant problems at work, school, or in other areas of your life due to these difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and organization?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m blaming everything on that, but it has caused real problems.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve missed follow-ups for interviews, which is embarrassing because I know those are important. I’m trying, but it’s like I drop the ball at the worst times. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your symptoms are causing significant distress or impairment in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have to say yes, even though I don’t love admitting that.\n\nIt’s not just annoying or inconvenient at this point.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do, unfortunately. I try not to, because I know it’s rude, but I catch myself jumping in before the other person has finished.

A lot of the time I think I know where they’re going, so I answer too early — and then they finish and I realize I either cut them off or answered the wrong question. It happens in conversations, and in interviews too, which is… not great. I’m not trying to be disrespectful, it’s more like my mouth gets ahead of me.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thanks for seeing me. I guess what finally made me come in is that I’m having a hard time keeping up with basic adult responsibilities. Like, I’ll forget to pay bills, or I won’t return interview emails in time, or I’ll miss appointments and not finish errands I meant to do.\n\nI know that probably sounds kind of irresponsible, and I don’t want it to come across like I don’t care, because I do. It’s just gotten to the point where it’s starting to cost me real opportunities, and I thought I should probably talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself struggling to focus or pay attention to details in your daily tasks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was careless on purpose, because I really was trying. But when I was doing biotech data work, I’d think a script or spreadsheet was all set, and then later I’d realize I had mislabeled a sample ID or missed a note in the protocol.\n\nSometimes I’d submit documentation and there would be really obvious typos that I just somehow didn’t see at the time. It’s embarrassing, because I would check things, but I guess not as carefully as I thought. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I try not to make it obvious. Like if I’m in an interview or a meeting, or even sitting through a movie, I get this kind of pressure inside my body, like I need to move or shift positions or do something with my hands.\n\nI can usually force myself to stay seated, but it feels really uncomfortable—almost like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I don’t move. I don’t know if that’s what you mean by restless?\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, sometimes. I don’t usually mean to be impulsive in a rude way, but in conversations or interviews I’ll think I know where the other person is going, and I’ll jump in before they’ve finished.\n\nThen I realize I either cut them off or answered a slightly different question than what they were actually asking. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to dominate the conversation or anything—I just react too fast sometimes, if that counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty organizing tasks and managing your time?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I do, more than I’d like to admit. I can usually start something with good intentions, like if someone gives me steps for a job application or a volunteer thing, or even just cleaning the apartment.\n\nAt first I’ll feel like, okay, I’ve got this. But then somehow I end up with half-finished forms, emails I meant to send but didn’t, or I’ll have moved things into piles and then not actually put them away. It’s not that I don’t care—I really do care. I just… lose the thread somewhere, I guess. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're unable to complete tasks or meet deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get anxious, I guess. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just falling apart all the time, but when I can’t finish something I was supposed to finish, I start feeling really overwhelmed and embarrassed.\n\nLike with job application steps, or a volunteer task, or even cleaning my apartment—I’ll start off pretty strong and think I’m handling it. Then somehow I’ll have half-finished forms, emails I meant to send but didn’t, or piles of stuff I moved around but never actually put away. And then the deadline gets closer, or I realize I haven’t followed through, and I kind of panic because it feels like I should’ve been able to just do it.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and organization, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there for a long time, honestly. I don’t think it just started recently.\n\nEven in elementary school, I remember teachers saying I was bright, which was nice, but then there’d always be comments like I lost worksheets a lot, or I rushed through directions, or I talked when I wasn’t supposed to. My desk was kind of… a disaster, too. Like, messy enough that I couldn’t find assignments sometimes.\n\nI guess I always hoped that was just me being a kid, but looking back, it does seem like the same kind of pattern. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're constantly on the go, or like you can't slow down your physical activity?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m, like, bouncing off the walls or anything. But I do feel like I’m almost always doing something.\n\nEven without having a job right now, I’ll move from one thing to the next all day—laundry, checking emails, reorganizing browser tabs, pacing around, starting dinner—and it feels weirdly hard to just stop. Like if I sit down, I remember something else I should do, so I get back up.\n\nThe frustrating part is a lot of it still doesn’t actually get finished, so it probably looks less productive than it feels in my head. But yes, I guess I do feel kind of constantly “on,” if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any difficulties in your relationships with friends, family, or romantic partners due to your symptoms?\n\nPatient: A little, yeah… maybe not as much in relationships exactly, but it does affect how connected I feel to people, if that makes sense.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve missed some follow-ups for interviews, and I had to step back from a volunteer project I actually really cared about because I wasn’t keeping up reliably. I feel bad saying that, because I don’t want it to sound like I don’t care or I’m not trying. It’s more like I can’t consistently manage the basic steps I’m supposed to do to move my life forward.\n\nAnd then I feel embarrassed, so I think I probably pull back from people or avoid explaining what happened. I’m not sure if that answers the relationship part correctly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant problems at work, school, or in other areas of your life due to these difficulties with attention, impulsivity, and organization?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m blaming everything on that, but it has caused real problems.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve missed follow-ups for interviews, which is embarrassing because I know those are important. And I had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about because I just wasn’t keeping up reliably.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and it feels like I can’t consistently manage the basic steps I’m supposed to take to get things moving again. I’m trying, but it’s like I drop the ball at the worst times. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say your symptoms are causing significant distress or impairment in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have to say yes, even though I don’t love admitting that.\n\nIt’s not just annoying or inconvenient at this point. Since getting fired, I’ve missed follow-ups for interviews, and I had to step back from a volunteer project I really cared about because I wasn’t keeping up reliably. So I’m still unemployed, and I feel like I can’t trust myself to manage the basic steps I’m supposed to be doing to get my life moving again.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “significant,” but it feels pretty distressing to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more related to not being able to focus or pay attention, or is it a mix of that and feeling restless or impulsive?\n\nPatient: I think it’s probably more the focus and attention part… or at least that’s the part I notice most.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down meaning to update my resume or do a coding practice problem, and then something small happens — a text notification, someone talking outside, or even just a random thought like, “Oh, I need to do that other thing” — and I’m completely pulled off track. Sometimes I don’t even realize I’ve switched away until a while later.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as distractibility, exactly, but it feels like I lose the thread really easily.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are mainly due to having trouble paying attention or following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: I think it’s both, but the following-through part is really what shows up.\n\nLike if someone gives me a set of steps for a job application, or a volunteer task, or even just cleaning the apartment, I’ll usually start out okay. I’ll feel like, “Yes, I can do this.” And then somehow I end up with half-finished forms, emails I meant to send but didn’t, or piles of stuff I moved around but never actually put away.\n\nI don’t mean I’m ignoring the instructions on purpose. It’s more like I lose the sequence after the first part, or I don’t get all the way to the finish. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are mainly due to having trouble paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… mainly paying attention, I guess.\n\nIt’s not that I’m trying not to listen or not do things. I’ll sit down to update my resume or work on a coding practice problem, and then something small happens — like a text notification, or someone talking outside, or even just a random thought about another task — and I’m completely off track before I really notice what happened.\n\nI know that probably sounds kind of normal, like everyone gets distracted, but for me it happens enough that it really messes with getting things done. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on a task that you lose track of everything else around you?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t think I’ve noticed that happening a lot.\n\nMaybe occasionally if I’m doing something I actually like, I can get absorbed for a bit, but I wouldn’t say I regularly lose track of everything around me. At least not that I can think of right now. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become so intensely focused on something that you tune out everything else?\n\nPatient: Not really, I don’t think so.\n\nI mean, maybe I can get into something for a little while if I like it, but I wouldn’t say I tune everything else out in a serious way. I haven’t really noticed that being a big issue for me. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with restlessness and impulsivity are as significant as your difficulties with attention and organization?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think they are, actually. Maybe I haven’t talked about that as much because it feels a little embarrassing, like I should be able to just sit still.\n\nBut when I have to sit through something — like an interview, or a meeting, or even a movie — I get this pressure inside, like I need to get up or shift around or do something with my hands. It’s not just boredom exactly. It feels like if I don’t move, I’m going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nSo… I guess attention and organization are a big problem, but the restlessness feels pretty significant too. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become so absorbed in an activity that you forget about everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing.\n\nI can get interested in something and spend more time on it than I meant to, maybe, but I don’t usually feel like I’ve forgotten everything around me. Or at least I haven’t noticed it that clearly. Sorry, I know that’s kind of a vague answer.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your ability to function socially or occupationally?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s been pretty hard.\n\nSince I got fired, I’ve missed some interview follow-ups — like emails or next steps I really meant to do — and then it makes me look unreliable, which feels awful. I also had to step back from a volunteer project I actually cared about, because I just wasn’t keeping up with what I said I would do.\n\nSo now I’m unemployed, and it kind of feels like I can’t reliably manage the basic steps to move my life forward. I know that sounds bad… but that’s honestly where I’m at.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others during conversations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, unfortunately. I try not to, because I know it’s rude, but I catch myself jumping in before the other person has finished.\n\nA lot of the time I think I know where they’re going, so I answer too early — and then they finish and I realize I either cut them off or answered the wrong question. It happens in conversations, and in interviews too, which is… not great. I’m not trying to be disrespectful, it’s more like my mouth gets ahead of me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

```json
    "traumatic_stressor": null,

    "additional_requirements": [
      "onset prior to age 12 confirmed",
      "symptoms causing significant distress or impairment in daily life"
    ]
  }
}
```
